# In Praise of Lord Ganesha

->**The Auspicious Beginning**<-

<note>The following woodcut illustrations represent the traditional invocation to Ganesha, the remover of obstacles, which is customary at the start of any significant literary or religious undertaking.</note>

[Illustration: At the top of the page, a woodcut depicts a <term>Purna Kalasha</term><note>A "full vessel" or overflowing vase, symbolizing abundance, wisdom, and the source of life.</note> filled with sacred foliage and flowers.]

<column-break/>

[Central Illustration: A detailed woodcut of the deity **Ganesha**. He is seated majestically beneath a ceremonial canopy. To his sides stand two female attendants, identified as his consorts **Riddhi** <note>Prosperity</note> and **Siddhi** <note>Spiritual Power</note>. One attendant holds a <term>chauri</term><note>A ceremonial fly-whisk made of yak tail hair, used as a symbol of royalty and divinity.</note> to honor the deity.]

<column-break/>

[Illustration: At the bottom of the page, a second woodcut of a <term>Purna Kalasha</term> mirrors the one at the top, completing the sacred frame.]

<margin>May there be no obstacles to this work.</margin>

*The entire composition is enclosed within an intricate floral vine border, representing the flourishing of knowledge and the beauty of the divine.*

<summary>This introductory page features woodcut illustrations of Ganesha flanked by his consorts and auspicious overflowing vases, serving as a traditional prayer for success and the removal of obstacles. It establishes a sacred and formal tone for the manuscript through classical Hindu iconography.</summary>

--- Page 2 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of the manuscript. It opens with traditional Sanskrit invocations and a poetic lineage of the author, Vishvanatha, who is providing a commentary on the "Sanjnatantra" (a treatise on astrological terminology) by Nilakantha.</meta>

# Salutations to the Illustrious Lord Ganesha. Salutations to the Illustrious Goddess Saraswati. Salutations to the Lotus Feet of the Illustrious Guru.

May that elephant-headed God <note>Ganesha</note> grant us prosperity; he who, having playfully grasped the skull-bowl of the Fierce Goddess <note>Chandi</note> with his trunk, makes it dance like a rhythmic drum in the wild dance of the Lord of Animals <note>Shiva</note>. He displays it within like a second solar disc, causing the Mother of the World <note>Parvati</note> to break into a smile. || 1 ||

There was a learned man named Divakara <note>meaning "The Sun"</note>, who was, I believe, a master of the vast calculations of the solar calendar. Through his own compositions, he illuminated the world like a second sun, revealing the universal form of knowledge to the eyes of men. || 2 ||

He had five sons who were equal to him, like the five celestial trees <note>Kalpavriksha</note> in the tradition of mathematical science. They were like lions in defeating the great elephants of opposing debaters, and like the five sacred fires in their performance of priestly duties. || 3 ||

Among them, the eldest was named Krishna, and the youngest was his brother. He was a man of virtuous conduct, whose speech was as pure as the infinite goddess of learning, and who was a teacher to the world. || 4 ||

From him was born the youngest son, who attained the highest rank among the assembly of the wise. His fame was constantly spread by his disciples and grand-disciples through the science of light <term>Jyotisha: the traditional Indian science of astronomy and astrology</term>. Known as Vishnu Jyotirvid, his virtues were recognized by kings; he was a leader among scholars on earth, and through his commentaries on great texts, he humbled the pride of even the teacher of the gods <note>Brihaspati</note>. || 5 ||

Then there was Mallari, who made the assembly of calculators his servants. He was a destroyer of the pride of others, a knower of various scriptures, and possessed an intellect as vast as a mountain. This Mallari was a master of self-restraint, a great orator in the world, and a clear-minded expert in all established astronomical truths <term>Siddhanta: the fundamental astronomical systems of India</term>. || 6 ||

His younger brother was named Keshava, who was like the moon to the ocean of joy for astrologers. He was an expert in speech, and with the nectar of his words, he brought delight to all the arts. || 7 ||

His younger brother is now Vishvanatha, who, by the grace of Lord Vishnu, is victorious through his virtues alone. He is an expert in the playful arts of the all-knowing, and has mastered all sciences under the guidance of his teacher, Nrisingha. || 8 ||

There was Nilakantha, a knower of the Vedanta, skilled in the traditional laws and the Vedas, and a destroyer of pride. To compose other great works, he entered the city of Kashi <note>Varanasi</note>, which is superior even to the explanations of the Lord of Serpents <note>Patanjali</note>. This Nilakantha, the son of Shri Ananta, composed the *Sanjnatantra*, a work difficult to grasp, which acts as a mirror showing both good and bad fortunes. || 9 ||

Vishvanatha, the astrologer who dwells in the region of the Godavari river, the foremost king of the Partha country, has composed this commentary on the *Sanjnatantra* in both prose and verse. Ever seeking the welfare of the world, he is the foremost among those who know ancient mathematics, yet he remains without pride, acting as a breaker of the knots <note>difficulties</note> in this text. || 10 ||

Performing these auspicious verses for the successful completion of the book without obstacles, he desires to... <unclear>m</unclear>

<summary>This opening page introduces the lineage of the scholar Vishvanatha, tracing his family of renowned astronomers and mathematicians back to Divakara, before introducing his commentary on Nilakantha's astrological treatise.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introductory commentary on the Tajika Neelakanthi, a seminal work on Perso-Arabic astrology adapted into Sanskrit. The author, Neelakantha, is establishing the lineage of his knowledge and beginning the systematic description of the zodiac signs.</meta>

<page-num>2</page-num>
<header>Chapter 2</header>
<sig>|| 2 ||</sig>

He understands. Sri Neelakantha, the author of this *Tajika* <note>Tajika refers to a system of Indian astrology influenced by Persian/Arabic traditions, specifically used for annual horoscopy</note>, reveals the *Tajika* taught by previous masters through beautiful verses, sound logic, various meters, and grammatical inflections. What kind of *Tajika*? One that brings joy to the minds of the learned. "Learned" refers to scholars; it brings pleasure to their inner hearts. The meaning is that by studying this text, a scholar's mind becomes delighted. Having done what? Having bowed to Heramba <note>A five-faced form of the god Ganesha, the remover of obstacles</note>. Furthermore, having bowed to Divakara, the Sun. Furthermore, having bowed to the lotus feet of the teacher named Ananta Daivajna <note>Ananta Daivajna was Neelakantha's father and teacher, a famous astronomer</note>. || 1 ||

A male, a large sign, "movable" by name, a fire sign, very firm, firm-limbed, four-footed, blood-red in color, hot—

-> Having bowed to Heramba and the Sun, as well as the lotus feet of the teacher Ananta, Sri Neelakantha explains this *Tajika* with beautiful verses to delight the minds of the learned. || 1 || Now he describes the nature of the twelve signs. First, Aries: Male, white-rayed <note>original: "shveta-rochi", likely referring to the brilliance of its ruling planet or stars</note>, very firm, four-footed, blood-red, hot, of the bile temperament, very loud, mountain-dwelling, fierce, yellow, powerful by day, ruler of the East, rising head-first, having little company and few offspring, dry, of the royal caste, and balanced—this is the Ram (Aries). || 2 || <-

Now he describes the nature of the sign Taurus. Taurus is female, an earth sign, cool, of a smooth body, fixed. Female, earth sign, cool, smooth-bodied, powerful at night, four-footed, white, very loud, rising head-first, of moderate offspring and company, the arms <note>referring to the body part associated with this sign in certain systems</note>, of the merchant caste. || 3 ||

Of a hot nature, "bile" refers to the bilious temperament. Very loud, "mountain-dwelling" means it roams the mountains. Fierce, "yellow" refers to its color. "Day" means it is strong during the daylight hours. Ruler of the East. "Little company and offspring" means he has little contact with women and few children. "Dry" means lacking in luster. "Royal" refers to the *Kshatriya* <term>Kshatriya: the warrior or ruling social class in traditional Indian society</term> caste. "Balanced"—the Ram (Aries) should be known by these qualities. Now, the sign is said to have two colors: blood-red and yellow. For Aries and Leo, two colors are mentioned because it was stated so by Samarasimha <note>An earlier authority on Tajika astrology frequently cited by Neelakantha</note>. || 2 ||

Female, "fixed" by name, a feminine sign, an earth sign, cool in nature, dry, lacking luster, ruler of the Southern direction—

<summary>Neelakantha opens his astrological treatise by honoring his father and the gods before defining the physical and temperamental characteristics of Aries and Taurus. He blends traditional Indian concepts like castes and temperaments with the Tajika system of annual divination.</summary>
<keywords>Neelakantha, Tajika, Aries, Taurus, Samarasimha, Ananta Daivajna, Indian astrology, zodiac characteristics</keywords>

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>This page continues the detailed astrological analysis of the zodiac signs (Rashis), following the description of Aries (Mesha). The text provides physical, elemental, and social characteristics for Gemini and Cancer.</meta>

<note>The scribe frequently uses the character 'pa' (प) in place of 'sha' (ष), a common orthographic variation in certain regional Sanskrit scripts.</note>

Good ground, moving on good ground, of a windy nature <note>original: "vayu-prakriti," referring to the Ayurvedic humor Vata</note>, strong at night, four-footed, white in color, making a great sound, rising unevenly <note>term: Vishamadaya; a technical term for signs that rise with their rear or side first</note>, having moderate offspring and moderate contact with women, auspicious, gentle, of the merchant class <note>term: Vaishya; the third social class in traditional Indian society</note>. This sign is large-limbed; this is stated by way of example for Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius. Others rising hereafter should also be understood as large-limbed. || 3 ||

The pair is the sign of Gemini; thus it should be known. It is the master of the western direction, a windy sign, having the luster of Venus <note>original: "shukra-bha," meaning it possesses a green or brilliant hue associated with the planet Venus/Shukra</note>, meaning it is green in color. It is two-footed <note>human</note>, a male sign, double-bodied or of a dual nature, meaning it possesses both movable and fixed qualities. Specifically, the first half is fixed and the latter half is movable. As stated by Bhattotpala <note>A famous 10th-century commentator on astrological texts</note>: "In a movable sign it is the opposite, or mixed; in the first half of the rising sign it is like a fixed sign, and in the latter half like a movable sign." It rises unevenly, is hot by nature, has moderate offspring and moderate contact with women, dwells in the forest, is of the servant class <note>term: Shudra; the fourth social class</note>, has a long sound <note>loud voice</note>, is oily/smooth, and is strong during the day. The word "tatha" is used to fill the meter. It is fierce and large-limbed. || 4 ||

->**Now the description of the nature of Gemini:**<-
->**West, windy, Venus-colored, two-footed, three-part, double-bodied, rising unevenly, hot; moderate offspring and contact, forest-dwelling, Shudra, long-sounding, oily, and given strength by the day. || 4 ||**<-

->**Now the description of the nature of Cancer:**<-
->**Having many offspring and many contacts, the Crab is a female sign, pale red, of low sound; auspicious, phlegmatic, oily, water-dwelling, rising evenly, Brahmin, strong at night, and lord of the North. || 5 ||**<-

The Crab is the sign of Cancer; it should be known as follows: having many offspring and many contacts with women. It is designated as "movable," a female sign, pale red <note>original: "patala," a pinkish-red like the trumpet flower</note>, quiet or of low sound, auspicious, gentle, of a phlegmatic nature <note>original: "kapha," the water/earth humor in Ayurveda</note>, oily, a watery sign, a water-dweller, rising evenly <note>term: Samodaya; rising in a balanced manner</note>, of the priestly class <note>term: Vipra/Brahmin</note>, strong at night, and master of the northern direction. It is large-limbed. || 5 ||

<summary>This page details the astrological characteristics of Gemini (Mithuna) and Cancer (Karka), defining their physical traits, elemental humors, social castes, and directional strengths. It includes a citation from the commentator Bhattotpala regarding the dual nature of certain signs.</summary>

<header>Chapter 1</header>

The Lion <note>Sanskrit: Harih or Simha; the zodiac sign Leo</note> is like a lion, unknowable, a male, a masculine person, fixed—meaning of a fixed nature—fiery like the crest of a flame, powerful during the day, yellow—meaning of a yellow complexion—rough—meaning lacking in luster—bilious—meaning of a bilious constitution <term>Pitta: one of the three humors in Ayurvedic medicine associated with fire and heat</term>—hot—meaning of a hot temperament—ruler of the East, firm—meaning of a firm body—four-footed, rising evenly, long-bodied, a deity of great sound, having little company or offspring—meaning little contact with women and few children—mountain-dwelling, a king—meaning of the warrior caste <term>Kshatriya</term>—fierce, smoky—meaning of a smoky color—a sign of wealth, and two-colored: yellow and smoky. || 6 || Such is the form of the Virgin <note>Sanskrit: Kanya; the zodiac sign Virgo</note>. Pale—meaning of a reddish-brown color—two-footed, female—a feminine sign—double-bodied—meaning of a dual nature, both moving and fixed...

### Now he describes the form of the sign Leo ||
The Lion is male, fixed, fire, diurnal, yellow, rough, bilious, hot, ruler of the East, firm, and four-footed. Rising evenly, long-bodied, ruled by the Sun, having little company or offspring, a mountain-dweller, a king, fierce, and smoky. || 6 ||

### Now he describes the form of the sign Virgo ||
Pale, two-footed, female, dual-natured, ruler of the South, nocturnal, windy, cold, rising evenly, and of the earth. Virgo is of half-sound, dwelling in auspicious lands, of the merchant caste, rough, with little company or offspring, and of auspicious speech. || 7 ||

### Now he describes the form of the sign Libra ||
The Scales <note>Sanskrit: Tula</note> are male, of varied colors, rising evenly, hot, blood-natured, windy, smooth-voiced, and of the West. Having very little offspring or company, gentle, powerful during the day, two-footed, and balanced. || 8 ||

...dual-natured, ruler of the South—meaning governor of the southern direction—powerful at night, of a windy constitution <term>Vata: the Ayurvedic humor associated with air and movement</term>, cold—meaning of a cold nature—rising evenly, of the earth—meaning an earth sign—half-sounded—meaning making broken sounds—dwelling in auspicious lands, of the merchant caste <term>Vaishya</term>, rough—meaning lacking luster—having little company or offspring—meaning little sexual union and few births—and an auspicious sign. The word "and" <note>Sanskrit: chakara</note> is used here to group these qualities together. || 7 || Male—a masculine sign—movable—meaning of a movable nature—variegated—meaning of many different colors—rising evenly, hot...

<meta>This page continues the systematic description of the zodiac signs (Rashis) and their physical, elemental, and social characteristics, following the previous descriptions of Virgo and Libra.</meta>

One who has few offspring and little sexual contact; of the Shudra <note>the laboring class</note> caste; fierce and cruel; possessing strength during the day; a biped <note>two-footed</note>; of even proportions and symmetrical limbs; and having a half-risen body: such is to be known as the sign **Libra**. || 8 || <margin>.</margin> Fixed <note>referring to a 'fixed' quality in astrology, suggesting stability</note>; white in color; a feminine sign; a watery sign; lord of the North direction; powerful at night; voiceless <note>original: 'nih-shabdah', meaning a silent sign</note>; many-footed; of a phlegmatic <term>Kapha|One of the three bodily humors in Ayurveda, associated with water and earth</term> constitution; rising evenly; a water-dweller; having excessive sexual contact and many offspring; auspicious and gentle; of a smooth or oily body; of the Brahmin <note>the priestly or intellectual class</note> caste: such is to be known as the sign **Scorpio**, which has a half-risen body. || 9 ||

---

**Now he describes the form of the sign Scorpio ||**
Fixed, white, feminine, watery, lord of the North, night-strong, voiceless, many-footed, and phlegmatic ||
Rising evenly, water-dwelling, with excessive contact and offspring, auspicious, smooth-bodied, a Brahmin is Scorpio || 9 || **Now he describes the form of the sign Sagittarius**
|| Masculine, gold-colored, mountain-dwelling, rising evenly, very noisy, day-strong, eastern, firm-bodied, yellow ||
Royal, hot-natured, bilious, Sagittarius has few offspring and little contact, dual-natured, biped, and fierce || 10 ||

---

Masculine sign; having the luster of gold; a mountain-dweller; rising evenly; very noisy <note>making a great sound</note>; powerful during the day; lord of the East direction; firm-limbed; rough <note>lacking luster or oilyness</note>; yellow in color; of the Kshatriya <note>the royal or warrior class</note> caste; of a hot temperament; of a bilious <term>Pitta|The bodily humor associated with fire and metabolic heat</term> constitution; said to have few offspring and little sexual contact; dual-natured <note>possessing both movable and fixed qualities</note>; a biped <note>two-footed</note>—though it should be known that the first half is two-footed and the second half is four-footed, as the author himself will explain later. An airy sign <note>original: 'agni', though contextually here it refers to the fire element</note>; fierce and cruel; of a golden or yellow hue: such is to be known as the sign **Sagittarius**. || 10 ||

The Deer <note>original: "Mriga"; refers to Capricorn</note>, the sign of the Sea-Monster <note>original: "Makara"</note>, is to be understood as follows: It is **Movable** <note>original: "Chara"; a quality indicating change and initiation</note>, of the **Earth element**, and associated with the **midnight** hour. It is the ruler of the **Southern direction** <note>original: "Yamasha"</note> and is a **Feminine** sign. Its color is **reddish-brown** <note>original: "Pingala"</note>; it is **dry** and lacks luster. It is considered **Benefic** <note>original: "Shubha"</note> and **terrestrial** (walking on the ground). Its nature is **cold**, and it is associated with **limited offspring** and limited intimacy with women. Its temperament is **Airy** <note>original: "Samira-Vata"</note>, and it is **strong at night**. In its **first half, it is four-footed**; the first half of the sign is known to have the nature of a quadruped. Regarding the second half, it will be stated later that it is a **water-dweller**. It has an **irregular ascent** <note>original: "Vishamodaya"; referring to how the sign rises over the horizon</note>. Its caste is **Merchant** <note>original: "Vaishya"</note>, and it represents the **place of wealth**. || 11 || The sign of the Water-Pot <note>original: "Kumbha"; Aquarius</note> is to be understood as follows: It is **footless** (without legs)...

---

-> Now he describes the nature of the Capricorn sign || <-

> The Deer-sign is movable, of the earth, midnight-strong, southern, reddish-brown, dry, benefic, terrestrial, and cold. It has limited offspring and intimacy, an airy nature, is four-footed in its first half, has an irregular ascent, and is of the Merchant caste. || 11 ||

-> Now he describes the nature of the Aquarius sign || <-

> The Water-Pot is footless, <insert>a hermaphrodite sign</insert>, strong in the day, moderate in intimacy and offspring, fixed, variegated in color, forest-dwelling, and of the air. It is smooth, hot, has a broken voice, balanced humors, is of the Servant caste, western, and has an irregular ascent. || 12 ||

-> Now he describes the nature of the Pisces sign || <-

> Pisces is footless, feminine, of the phlegmatic humor, a water sign, night-strong, silent, tawny, of a dual nature, and water-dwelling. It is smooth, very fertile, of the Priest caste, benefic, northern, and has an irregular ascent. || 13 || || ||

---

It is a **Hermaphrodite sign** <note>original: "Napumsaka"; neither strictly male nor female</note>, **strong during the day**, and has **moderate intimacy and offspring**. It is **Fixed** <note>original: "Sthira"; indicating stability</note> and **variegated in color**. It is a **forest-dweller** and an **Airy sign**. It is **smooth** (oily), **hot in nature**, and has a **broken voice**. Its **humors are balanced** <note>original: "Tulya-dhatu"; a balance of Vata, Pitta, and Kapha</note>. Its caste is **Servant** <note>original: "Shudra"</note>, it rules the **Western direction**, has an **irregular ascent**, and is fierce/cruel. It represents the **place of wealth**. || 12 || The sign of the Fish <note>original: "Meena"; Pisces</note> is to be understood as follows: It is **footless**, a **Feminine sign**, and of a **phlegmatic temperament** <note>original: "Kapha"</note>. It is a **Water sign**, **strong at night**, and **silent** (without sound). Its color is **tawny** <note>original: "Vabhru"</note>. It is of **Dual nature** <note>original: "Dvitannu"; mutable</note> and **dwells in water**. It is **smooth**, **highly fertile**, and belongs to the **Priest caste** <note>original: "Vipra/Brahmin"</note>. It is **Benefic**, rules the **Northern direction**, has an **irregular ascent**, and represents the **place of wealth**. || 13 ||

<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of zodiac signs, focusing on their elemental qualities, social castes, and physical temperaments. It establishes the rules for compatibility between different signs based on their elemental groupings.</meta>

The nature of this sign is watery; it is a **water sign** <note>original: "jala-rashi"</note>. It is moist, smooth, and characterized by a strong tendency toward procreation and a deep attachment to women. The word "also" implies a combination of these traits. Its caste is **Brahmin** <note>the priestly or scholarly class</note>.

It is auspicious, gentle, and rules the northern direction. It rises irregularly <note>original: "vishamadoya," referring to the astronomical timing of the sign's appearance on the horizon</note> and possesses a share of noble fame. || 13 ||

### On Elemental Friendships and Enmities

Now, having described the nature of the signs, the author explains the divisions of friendship and enmity among them:

-> **Friendship exists between the groups of Earth and Water, and between Fire and Air. Otherwise, there is enmity.** <-

-> **The latter half of Sagittarius is four-footed; the first half of Capricorn is aquatic.** <- || 14 ||

The friendship between the **Earth signs** and **Water signs** is natural. Similarly, the friendship between the **Fire signs** and **Air signs** should be understood. In contrast to this, there is a state of enmity <note>original: "ari-bhava"</note>. This means that when considering the compatibility between a father and son, a woman and man, or a master and servant, friendship should be predicted if their signs belong to the Earth and Water groups.

The same applies to the Fire and Air groups. Otherwise, one must declare enmity. For example, Earth and Fire, or Earth and Air, are inimical groups. If both individuals belong to the same elemental group (e.g., both are Earth), then there is a state of great mutual affection.

Furthermore, while discussing the nature of the signs, the author applies the **"Lion’s Glance" rule** <note>term: "Simhavalokana-nyaya" — a Sanskrit metaphorical principle where one looks back at what was previously stated while moving forward, like a lion pausing to look behind</note> to clarify specific forms for Sagittarius and Capricorn. In the latter half of Sagittarius, it is considered **four-footed** <note>quadrupedal</note>. In the first half of Capricorn, it is considered **aquatic** <note>water-dwelling</note>. || 14 ||

### On the Humors and Castes

To explain the results of these sign characteristics, the author briefly describes their physical temperaments <note>term: "Doshas" — the three bodily humors in Ayurvedic medicine</note>:

-> **Bile, Wind, Mixed, and Phlegm: these follow the signs in groups of three. The Warrior, Merchant, Laborer, and Priest castes also follow the signs. All results should be determined according to the sign.** <- || 15 ||

The temperaments are assigned as follows:
*   **Aries:** Bile <note>Pitta</note>
*   **Taurus:** Wind <note>Vata/Anila</note>
*   **Gemini:** Mixed <note>Dhatu-sama; a balance of all three humors</note>
*   **Cancer:** Phlegm <note>Kapha</note>

This cycle repeats: Leo is Bile, Virgo is Wind, Libra is Mixed, and Scorpio is Phlegm. Likewise, Sagittarius is Bile, Capricorn is Wind, and Aquarius is Mixed...

<summary>This page details the elemental compatibility between zodiac signs, noting that Earth/Water and Fire/Air are natural allies. It also categorizes the signs by their Ayurvedic temperaments (Bile, Wind, Phlegm) and social castes to predict interpersonal relationships.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of astrological classifications and begins the mathematical section on calculating the solar return (Varshaphala).</meta>

<page-num>4</page-num>
<header>Chapter 1</header>

From Pisces <note>original: "mina"</note> onwards—starting from Aries and ending with Pisces—by dividing them into groups of four, a triple rotation is formed. These zodiac signs <term>Rashis: the twelve signs of the zodiac</term> should be known and understood by the learned scholars. Now, the triple rotation of the four castes—the Warrior, Merchant, Laborer, and Priest—among the twelve signs should be understood. It is as follows:

->**The Caste Classifications of the Signs**<-

| Sign | Caste |
| :--- | :--- |
| Aries | Warrior <note>Kshatriya</note> |
| Taurus | Merchant <note>Vaishya</note> |
| Gemini | Laborer <note>Shudra</note> |
| Cancer | Priest <note>Brahmin</note> |
| Leo | Warrior |
| Virgo | Merchant |
| Libra | Laborer |
| Scorpio | Priest |
| Sagittarius | Warrior |
| Capricorn | Merchant |
| Aquarius | Laborer |
| Pisces | Priest |

The results for all these should be interpreted according to the nature and characteristics of the signs. || 15 ||

Now the mathematics will be discussed. First, the movement of the planets will be explained. For this movement, the commencement of the year <note>Varsha-pravritti</note> is required. That has not been stated here by the author of this book.

---

### Now, the method for determining the time of the Year-Entrance for the purpose of calculating the Year-Fruit:
### The elapsed years, increased by their fourth part, multiplied by twenty-one; then add forty <note>Khaveda: 'Kha' (0) and 'Veda' (4) represents 40 in the Katapayadi system</note> ghatis <term>Ghati: a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</term> and the weekday of birth. The result, divided by seven, indicates the weekday and time of the Year-Entrance. || 16 ||

Why is this mentioned? Because it is required for the Year-Fruit <note>Varshaphala</note>, and since it is useful here, we are demonstrating it. Furthermore, the determination of the planetary periods <note>Dashas</note>, sub-periods, month-entrances, and day-entrances are also mentioned in the Year-Fruit system; however, since they are explained in that specific context, they are not fully detailed here. We will demonstrate them wherever they are applicable.

**The Calculation:** "Elapsed years" <note>Gata-samah</note> refers to the years passed. For example, the Era <term>Shaka: the historical calendar era used in India</term> at birth should be subtracted from the current Era. The remainder is the elapsed years. These should be "increased by their fourth part" <note>divided by 4 and added back</note>. "Nature" <note>Prakriti</note> refers to the number twenty-one; the years should be multiplied by this. To this "Year-count," add forty *ghatis*. Then, add the weekday and the desired time <note>ghatis and palas</note> of the original birth. Finally, divide the resulting figure by seven. The remainder indicates the weekday, *ghatis*, and *palas* of the Year-Entrance <note>the solar return</note> for the current year in this text. || 16 ||

The number of elapsed years <note>the count of years passed since birth</note> is multiplied by eleven <note>original: "Shivaghna," where Shiva represents the number 11</note>. To this, add its own portion divided by one hundred and seventy <note>original: "svakhāṃṣṭrīṃdulavāḍhyaḥ," a numerical code for 170</note>. This result should then be added to the birth lunar day <note>the specific day of the lunar month when the person was born</note>, counting from the first day of the bright fortnight of the month of Magha. The total is then divided by thirty <note>original: "khāgni," where 'kha' is 0 and 'agni' is 3, representing 30</note>. In that resulting lunar day, the Year-Entry <term>Varshapravesha: the exact moment the sun returns to its natal position</term> occurs. However, there is no absolute rule that it must fall on that exact lunar day; it may sometimes occur on the preceding or following day. Here, the weekday is the true authority. Among the three possible lunar days, the one on which the previously calculated weekday falls is to be known as the true day of Year-Entry. This is how the year is arrived at; the remainder should be understood as the calculation of the planets. Now, the sequence of examples is written. First, a birth record is provided.

### Now he describes the means of determining the lunar day: The years multiplied by eleven, increased by its 170th part, divided by thirty, and added to the birth lunar day, results in—

-> the Year-Entry. || 27 || <-

The example is written: In the Year <note>Samvat</note> 1648, Shaka era 1513, on the first day of the bright fortnight of the month of Magha, a Wednesday. The time was 36 <term>Ghatis: a unit of 24 minutes</term>, under the Shravana constellation, 54 Ghatis, and the Vajra Yoga, 1 Ghati. On this auspicious day, at 27 Ghatis and 53 <term>Palas: a unit of 24 seconds</term> from sunrise, and 12 Ghatis and 31 Palas into the night, the birth occurred. The shadow of the gnomon <note>used for latitude</note> was 7.30. The longitudinal correction <note>Deshantara</note> was 30. The Sun's position was 9 signs, 7 degrees, 30 minutes, and 6 seconds. The rising sign at birth <note>Lagna</note> was 5 signs, 15 degrees, 35 minutes, and 50 seconds. Now, the example for the Year-Entry: The Shaka year at birth, 1513, is subtracted from the current Shaka year, 1550. The remainder is 37, which is the number of elapsed years. This, increased by its fourth part (18.50) <note>likely referring to leap year adjustments or specific cycle fractions</note>, becomes 46.15.50. The elapsed years (37) multiplied by twenty-one equals 777. This is divided by forty, yielding 19 Ghatis, 25 Palas, and 30 seconds. This, added to the previous sum of 46.15.50, results in 46.34.25.30. This is then added to the birth weekday and time (4.30.53), resulting in the following figure:

This is multiplied by the elapsed years, 37, resulting in 13514 / 34 / 25 / 30. When the birth weekday and time, 4 | 37 / 53, are added to this, the sum is 13519 / 12 / 18 / 8 / 2 / 0. **Now, the calculation of the lunar day <term>Tithi: a lunar day, 1/30th of a lunar month</term>.** The count of elapsed years, 37, is multiplied by eleven (11), resulting in 407. This is increased by 2, which is the quotient from dividing by its own 170th part, resulting in 409. Then, the 2 elapsed lunar days starting from the bright first day at the time of birth are added, making 411. When divided by thirty, the remainder is 21. This results in the 6th day of the dark fortnight at the year's entry. Because the previously calculated weekday does not fall on this lunar day, but on the next, one (1) is added to this lunar day (21), making 22: the 7th day of the dark fortnight.

In whatever month the birth occurs, the year's entry usually occurs in that same month. Sometimes it occurs in the month before or after the birth month; the proof for that will be stated. When the Sun is exactly equal to the Sun at the time of birth, that month should be known as the month of the year's entry. In the year 1685 of the <term>Vikram Samvat: an Indian calendar era starting in 57 BCE</term> (Shaka era 1550), on the 7th day of the dark fortnight of the month of Magha, on Monday, at 37 <term>Ghatis: a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</term> and 39 <term>Palas: a unit of time equal to 24 seconds</term>; the Hasta Nakshatra is at 17 ghatis and 28 palas; the Sukarma Yoga is at 27 ghatis and 28 palas. On this auspicious day, at 12 ghatis, 18 palas, and 30 vipalas after sunrise, the 38th year begins.

**Now, he states the proof for the time of this year's entry:**

-> "At that moment, the Sun is exactly equal to the Sun at the time of birth. That very month should be known by the wise as the year's entry, certainly." <-

Because of this reason, the Sun calculated at the time of the year's entry according to the prescribed method is equal—in signs, degrees, minutes, and seconds—to the Sun at the time of birth. This is the proof of the time of the year's entry. An example of this is shown here. The astronomer Ganesha Daivajna, in his work *Tajika Bhushana*, realized that the Sun at the year's entry does not always come out exactly equal in minutes to the Sun at birth, and so, out of confusion, he mentioned another corrective calculation. That verse says: "When the Shaka year of birth is subtracted from the current Shaka year, the remaining elapsed years are multiplied by sixteen and five..." <note>The text cuts off here, describing a specific mathematical formula from Ganesha Daivajna's treatise.</note>

Divided by the number of elephants <note>original: "nāgai", a numerical code for 8</note> in the age <note>original: "yuge", referring to 4</note>. The result obtained, when added to the day and hour of birth and divided by seven, the remainder indicates the fixed point of the Year Entry <term>Varshaparvesha: the exact moment the sun returns to its natal position</term>. || 1 ||

At the time of the Year Entry, measured in hours and minutes <note>original: "ghati-pala"</note>, the position is made clearer and updated. The Sun's position should be calculated according to the methods of the ancient astronomical treatises <term>Siddhanta: traditional Indian astronomical systems</term> to match the human solar year. || 2 ||

If the "Lord of the Day-Lily" <note>The Sun</note> at the time of the Year Entry does not exactly match the minutes of the Sun at the time of birth, the difference between the two, multiplied by the daily motion and added or subtracted, must be applied. || 3 ||

From this fixed point, the true position is produced. At that moment, the astrological houses <term>Bhava</term> and the planets <term>Kheta</term> are determined for the judgment of results. || 4 ||

### Now, an example is shown to remove the confusion of Ganesha Daivajna.

At the time of birth, according to the *Siddhanta Rahasya*, the count of days <term>Ahargana: total days elapsed since the start of the epoch</term> is 2128. The cycles <note>revolutions</note> are 6. The mean Sun at sunrise is 9 signs, 6 degrees, 17 minutes, and 43 seconds. Adding the longitudinal correction <term>Deshantara</term> of 22 seconds, it becomes 9 | 6 | 18 | 5. Adjusted by the desired time <note>time elapsed since sunrise</note> of 37 hours and 53 minutes, the Sun at that moment is 9 | 6 | 45 | 25. The anomaly <term>Mandakendra</term> is 5 | 11 | 14 | 35. The equation of center <term>Mandaphala</term> is positive 0 | 42 | 23. The corrected Sun is 9 | 7 | 27 | 48. The precession <term>Ayanamsha</term> is 17 | 49. The ascensional difference parts <note>Chara-khandas</note> are 75 | 6 | 0 | 25. The positive ascensional difference <note>Charadhana</note> is 138. Thus, the true Sun at the time of birth is 9 | 7 | 39 | 0 | 6.

Now, at the time of the Year Entry, the *Siddhanta Rahasya* day-count is 3595. The cycles are 9. The mean Sun at sunrise is 9 | 6 | 13 | 58. Adding the longitudinal correction, it is 9 | 6 | 13 | 20. Adjusted by the desired time of 12 | 17 | 8 | 20, the Sun at that moment is 9 | 6 | 45 | 27. The anomaly is 5 | 12 | 14 | 33. The equation of center is positive 0 | 42 | 13. The corrected Sun is 9 | 7 | 27 | 40. The precession is 18 | 26. The ascensional difference parts are 75 | 6 | 0 | 25. The positive ascensional difference is 136. Thus, the true Sun at the time of the Year Entry is 9 | 7 | 39 | 0 | 6.

This Sun at the time of the year return has become exactly equal to the Sun previously calculated for the time of birth. By this, the opinion of Ganesha Daivajna is refuted <note>The author argues that Ganesha's method for calculating the solar return was flawed or unnecessary</note>. It is understood that Ganesha Daivajna did not observe that equality could be reached through this other method. They <note>Ganesha and his followers</note> brought the Sun into equality with the birth Sun by adjusting the Sun's motion found in the almanac <term>Panchanga</term> using the true hours. If it is argued that another refinement was mentioned for the visual Year Entry, we reply: the reason for that is also stated here. We shall demonstrate the difference that falls between the Solar and Brahma systems, and so on.

# Section 2 || 6 ||

When there is a discrepancy between different astronomical schools <note>original: "pakṣa"</note>, the calculation of the Sun at the time of birth and the Sun as recorded in the almanac <note>term: "Panchang" - a traditional Hindu calendar and almanac</note> is performed, and a difference arises between them. Now, if one objects: "The Sun at birth was calculated according to the Solar School <note>term: "Saura-paksha" - the astronomical tradition based on the Surya Siddhanta</note>, and the Sun in the almanac also follows the Solar School, so why is there still a difference?" The reason is stated: When a major calculation is made for someone's birth, the corrections for the local latitude <note>term: "Chara" - ascensional difference</note> and longitude <note>term: "Deshantara" - terrestrial longitude</note> of their specific birth village are applied. However, the person who created the almanac applied the corrections for a different location, such as the Marwar region <note>original: "Marudesha"</note>. Because the corrections for latitude and longitude differ from village to village, a discrepancy arises. If the corrections for the same location were applied to both, this difference would not occur. From whichever text the Sun is calculated, starting from the birth time, through various corrections...

---

Now, the true positions of the planets <note>term: "Spashta-graha" - the mathematically corrected 'true' longitude of a planet as seen from Earth</note> are to be calculated at the moment of the Solar Return <note>term: "Varshapravesha" - the entry of the Sun into the same zodiacal point as birth, marking the start of a new year</note>. For the sake of ease, the author describes the "motion adjustment" <note>term: "Chalana"</note> for the planets found in the almanac:

Multiply the daily motion of the planet by the days elapsed or remaining. Divide the result by sixty (60). The resulting quotient in minutes and seconds should be subtracted or added; thus, the planet becomes "true" (accurate). || 18 ||

---

The Sun and other planets would then be accurate:

| 2 | 5 | 8 | 8 | 8 | 7 | 6 | 3 |

| 20 | 9 | 21 | 34 | 21 | 48 | 54 | 29 |
| 6 | 47 | 36 | 58 | 13 | 48 | 54 | 26 |
| 61 | 85 | 44 | 59 | 13 | 70 | 3 | 3 |
| 17 | 24 | 1 | 23 | 50 | 7 | 11 | 11 |

Corrected: The Sun calculated from that text at the moment of the Solar Return becomes identical down to the second <note>original: "vikalā"</note>; this is certain. Knowing that equality between the two is achieved in this way, the "Crest-Jewel of Astrologers" <note>a title for a master scholar</note> has shown this very example.
The venerable teacher Keshavadaivajña <note>a famous 15th-century astronomer</note> has mentioned another correction at the time of the Solar Return; what was said by Ganesha Daivajña <note>Keshava's son and author of the Graha Laghava</note> is [considered by some here to be] incorrect.

What is the daily motion <note>original: "kāgati"</note>? Multiply it by the movement factors <note>the time elapsed since the epoch</note> and divide by sixty. The resulting degrees, minutes, and seconds should be subtracted from the planet's position as listed in the almanac <note>original: "pañcāṅga"</note> if the difference is large; if the difference is small, it should be added. This results in the "True Planet" <term>Spashta-graha: the actual observed position of a celestial body after applying corrections to its mean position</term> for that specific moment. For planets in retrograde motion <note>moving backward from our perspective on Earth</note> and for Rahu and Ketu <note>the lunar nodes</note>, the calculation should be understood as the opposite of the standard direction.

**Example:**
The time of the solar return <note>original: "Varshapravesha"</note> is 2 days, 12 hours, and 18 minutes. Subtracting the epoch time of 6 days, 23 hours, and 56 minutes gives the "motion digits" of 2, 58, and 22. Using these, all planets are calculated for the exact moment of the solar return.

**Mars:** Mean: 8s 1° 12' 18". Fast Anomaly <note>original: "shīghra-kendra"</note>: 0s 25° 25' 9". Equation: 4° 56' 56". Corrected Mars: 8s 16° 9' 14". Slow Anomaly <note>original: "mṛdu-kendra"</note>: 7s 13° 42' 46". True Mars: 8s 12° 5' 10" 36'''. Daily motion: 4' 8" 16'''.

**Mercury:** Anomaly: 8s 7° 44' 22". Equation: 10° 36' 10". Corrected Mercury: 8s 26° 12' 27". Slow Anomaly: 10s 35° 50' 33". True Mercury: 8s 12° 22' 39". Daily motion: 6' 9" 8'''.

**Jupiter:** 9s 4° 8' 40". Anomaly: 0s 23° 6' 47". Equation: 0° 12' 32". Corrected Jupiter: 9s 4° 21' 12". Slow Anomaly: 8s 25° 38' 17". True Jupiter: 8s 29° 52' 42". Daily motion: 12' 24'''.

**Venus:** Anomaly: 8s 29° 13' 42". Equation: 20° 39' 44". Corrected Venus: 8s 19° 6' 54". Slow Anomaly: 6s 23° 54' 16". True Venus: 7s 25° 50' 55". Daily motion: 7' 2'''.

**Saturn:** Mean Saturn: 5s 24° 16' 26". Anomaly: 2s 12° 22' 11". Equation: 2° 52' 0". Corrected Saturn: 5s 27° 8' 26". Slow Anomaly: 2s 25° 23' 45". True Saturn: 6s 7° 54' 14" 21'''. These are the values...

## Chapter 1

Even though the planets exist, their degrees are obtained later through the calculation of aspects <note>original: "drishaya"</note> and the Sahamas <note>Arabic-influenced sensitive points in Tajika astrology</note>; therefore, they are taken from those calculations. || 18 || At the time of the solar return <term>Varshapravesha: the exact moment the Sun returns to its natal position</term>, the lunar mansion <term>Nakshatra: one of the 27 divisions of the sky</term> is noted. One should calculate the elapsed time <note>ghatikas</note> of that Nakshatra up to the desired time. These are multiplied by sixty and divided by the total duration of the Nakshatra <note>bhabhoga</note> added to the elapsed and remaining portions. The result obtained is multiplied by sixty and added to the count of Nakshatras starting from Ashvini <note>the first Nakshatra</note>. These become the time units up to the current Nakshatra. These are divided by nine; the quotient gives the degrees <note>amsha</note>. The remainder is multiplied by sixty and divided by nine to get the minutes <note>kala</note>. Again, the remainder is multiplied by sixty and divided by nine to get the seconds <note>vikala</note>. In this way, the signs, degrees, and so on are determined for that specific moment.

Now, the calculation of motion <note>gati</note>. The number 48,000 <note>original: "khakha-bhrashta-veda"</note> is divided by the total duration of the Nakshatra. This gives the clear motion of the Moon in minutes and seconds.

**Example:** At the time of the solar return, the Nakshatra is Hasta. Its elapsed time on the previous day was 44;24. This is added to the desired time of 12;18.
<gloss>56;42</gloss>
Now he describes the calculation of the Moon and the Nakshatra from the almanac <note>Panchanga</note>. Sixty multiplied by the elapsed time, divided by the total duration, and multiplied by the numerical factors... <note>The text here details a specific mathematical formula involving the number 9</note>. The Moon's position in degrees and its true motion are found by dividing 48,000 by the Nakshatra duration. || 21 ||

Up to the point of calculation, the elapsed time of the Hasta Nakshatra is 53;42. This is multiplied by sixty to get 3222. Divided by the total duration of 58;52, the degrees are obtained. The dividend is 193,320 and the divisor is 3532. The result is 54;44;12. This is multiplied by 12 (the number of Nakshatras passed starting from Ashvini) and then by sixty to get 720. Adding the previous result of 54;44;12 gives 774;44;12. Doubled, this is 1549;28;24. Divided by nine, the result in degrees etc. is 172;9;49. These degrees are divided by thirty to find the sign <note>Rashi</note>. The resulting instantaneous Moon is 5 signs, 22 degrees, 9 minutes, and 47 seconds <note>Virgo 22° 9' 47"</note>.

Now for the calculation of motion. 48,000 divided by the Nakshatra duration <margin>|| 17 ||</margin> of 58;52 gives the clear motion of the Moon as 815;24. In the manner described, the instantaneous clear positions of the planets are established. || 21 ||

# Calculation of the Houses and Junctions through Verses in Paper-Script.

Herein is the calculation of the <term>Ascendant</term> <note>Sanskrit: Lagna; the point of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon</note>, the calculation of the desired time, the calculation of the <term>Tenth House</term> <note>Sanskrit: Dashama-lagna; the Midheaven or Zenith</note>, and the calculation of the <term>Hour Angle</term> <note>Sanskrit: Nata; the angular distance from the meridian</note>. Among these, he first describes the calculation of the Hour Angle. When the desired time is before midday, it should be subtracted from the elapsed ghatis <note>A ghati is a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</note> of the half-day; then it becomes the "East Hour Angle" of the day. At night, when the desired time is before midnight, it should be subtracted from the elapsed ghatis of the half-night; then it becomes the "East Hour Angle" of the night. When the desired time is after midday, the half-day portion should be subtracted from the desired ghatis elapsed since sunrise; this becomes the "West Hour Angle" of the day. Similarly, when the time is after midnight, the half-night portion should be subtracted from the desired ghatis elapsed since sunset; this becomes the "West Hour Angle" of the night. || 20 || The <term>True Sun</term> <note>Sanskrit: Tatkalika-arka; the sun's position at the specific moment</note> should be added to the <term>Precession of the Equinoxes</term> <note>Sanskrit: Ayanamsha</note>. From the multiplication of its elapsed or remaining degrees: when

> The East Hour Angle is the half-day or half-night portion minus the elapsed ghatis to be subtracted. Subtracting the half-day or half-night portion from the desired ghatis of day or night results in the West Hour Angle. || 20 || From the multiplication of the elapsed or remaining degrees of the <term>Sun with Precession</term> <note>Sanskrit: Sayana-arka</note> at that time || by its own rising time and divided by thirty (30), the result is what is elapsed or remaining; this should be subtracted from the Sun's position. || 21 || || || || || ||

the <term>Negative Ascendant</term> <note>Sanskrit: Rina-lagna; used in specific retrograde or reverse calculations</note> is to be calculated, then the elapsed degrees should be taken. When the <term>Positive Ascendant</term> <note>Sanskrit: Dhana-lagna</note> is being calculated, then the remaining degrees should be taken. The rising time of the sign <note>Sanskrit: Svodaya</note> should be multiplied by these and divided by thirty. The result, whether it be the Sun's elapsed or remaining portion, should be subtracted from the desired ghatis. Thereafter, from the remainder, one should calculate the ascendant using the upcoming rising times. Similarly, when the elapsed time is to be calculated, the previous rising times should be subtracted from the multiplied rising time. When the remaining time is to be calculated, the subsequent rising times should be subtracted from the multiplied rising time. The remainder of the subtraction is multiplied by thirty (30). That which cannot be further subtracted is the <term>Incomplete Rising</term> <note>Sanskrit: Ashuddhodaya</note>. The result obtained by dividing by that should be taken as degrees and so on. This should be subtracted from the incomplete sign or added to the completed sign. # Section 1

Subtract the degrees and minutes from the mid-heaven <note>original: "havyamadhye," referring to the meridian or tenth house</note>, and then the precession of the equinoxes <term>Ayanamsha: the longitudinal difference between the tropical and sidereal zodiacs</term> should be subtracted. This results in the Ascendant <term>Lagna: the degree of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon</term>. In another way, using the "Lanka" rising times <term>Lankodaya: rising times of signs at the terrestrial equator</term>, one should calculate the elapsed and remaining portions of the Sun's position.

Convert these into minutes of arc and subtract them from the western "natakala" <note>the hour angle or time from the meridian</note>. The remaining operations should be performed as before to find the Tenth House. Specifically: when there is an eastern hour angle, treat that as the desired time, use the equatorial rising times, and subtract the Sun's position; the remaining calculation is subtractive. When there is a western hour angle, treat that as the desired time, use the equatorial rising times, and subtract the Sun's position; the remaining calculation is additive. This results in the Tenth House.

Now, the method for determining the other houses from the Tenth House and the Ascendant is described. It is said: "By adding seven signs..." The Ascendant plus six signs <note>original: "rashishatku," six signs or 180 degrees</note> becomes the seventh house <note>the descendant</note>. Similarly, the fourth house <note>the Nadir or Imum Coeli</note> is determined. Thus, the four angular houses <term>Kendra: the 1st, 4th, 7th, and 10th houses</term> are established.

> From the minutes of the desired time, subtract the local rising times of the signs passed and to be passed. || Take the remainder, multiply by 30, and divide by the rising time of the next sign to get degrees and minutes. || 22 ||
>
> Subtract or add the precession of the equinoxes as required. Similarly, using equatorial rising times, subtract the elapsed and remaining portions from the time-minutes. || 23 ||
>
> From the eastern or western hour angle, the Tenth House is found just like the Ascendant. By adding six signs to the Ascendant, the seventh house is found; from the Tenth, the fourth is found. || 24 ||
>
> One-sixth of the difference <note>between two houses</note> added to the Ascendant creates the "junction." By adding that sixth part again and again, the three junctions and the houses are formed. || 25 ||

Thus, four houses are known. Now, to find the houses between the Ascendant and the fourth house: subtract the Ascendant from the fourth house. One-sixth of that remainder (in signs, degrees, etc.) should be added to the Ascendant. This becomes the "junction" <term>Sandhi: the transition point or cusp between two astrological houses</term> between the Ascendant and the second house. This is the end of the first house and the beginning of the second house; this rule applies everywhere.

By adding that same sixth part again to the junction, the second house is found. The second house plus the sixth part becomes the junction between the second and third houses. That junction plus the sixth part becomes the third house. That third house plus the sixth part becomes the junction between the third and fourth houses. In this way, the three junctions and the houses are produced. After that...

<summary>This page provides mathematical formulas for calculating the Tenth House (Midheaven) using hour angles and equatorial rising times, and details the "Shashtamsha" method for dividing the quadrants into three houses each to complete the twelve-house system.</summary>
<keywords>Ascendant (Lagna), Midheaven (Dashama), House Cusps (Sandhi), Precession (Ayanamsha), Equatorial Rising (Lankodaya), Mathematical Astronomy, Jyotisha</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for calculating astrological houses and the ascendant (lagna), following the determination of the initial house divisions.</meta>

The remaining three houses and their <term>junctions (sandhis)</term> <note>The 'sandhi' is the transition point or cusp between two astrological houses</note> are to be calculated as follows: by subtracting the previously determined sixth-part value and adding it to the fourth house, the junction between the fourth and fifth houses is found. Adding it again gives the fifth house itself. Adding it once more gives the junction between the fifth and sixth houses. Adding it again gives the sixth house. Finally, adding it again gives the junction between the sixth and seventh houses. Thus, the six houses with their junctions are produced. By adding six signs (180 degrees) to these, the remaining six houses and their junctions are determined. Now, the purpose of establishing these house junctions is explained: **"The Planet..."** When a planet is situated in a house and its position in signs, degrees, and minutes exactly matches the center of that house, it gives its full result—whether good or bad—rated at twenty units <note>original: "vimsati-vimbam," a traditional scale of 20 points for planetary strength</note>. If the planet is exactly at the junction (sandhi), its result is zero.

---

> The next three houses are likewise calculated; by adding six signs, the other six are found.
> When a planet equals the house center, its fruit is full; at the junction, it is nil. || 26 ||

From the desired time of birth, one should calculate the difference between the rising times of the signs. If the sun is subtracted or added, that becomes the <term>ascendant (lagna)</term> <note>The sign rising on the eastern horizon at the time of birth</note>. For a birth at night, the ascendant is calculated by adding six signs to the sun's position. || 27 ||

---

-> || 21 || 22 || 23 || 24 || 25 || 26 || <-

Now, if the <term>elapsed portion (bhukta)</term> or the <term>remaining portion (bhogya)</term> of the Sun—calculated according to the rules—cannot be subtracted from the hours and minutes of the desired time, then by what means should the ascendant be calculated? To this, he says: **"The elapsed..."** If the elapsed or remaining portion cannot be subtracted from the desired time's hours and minutes, then those hours and minutes should be <term>multiplied by thirty</term> and divided by the rising time of the <term>tropical Sun</term> <note>Sayana-surya: the sun's position adjusted for the precession of the equinoxes</note>. The resulting degrees and minutes should be subtracted from or added to the Sun. Specifically, if the "elapsed" portion was used, the result is subtracted from the Sun; if the "remaining" portion was used, the result is added to the Sun. That becomes the ascendant. For calculating the ascendant at night, the calculation is performed using the Sun's position plus six signs. Even for calculating the <term>tenth house (midheaven)</term> at night, it is derived from the Sun plus six signs.

<summary>This page details the mathematical methods for determining the twelve astrological houses and their junctions, explaining that planetary influence is strongest at the house center and weakest at the boundaries. It also provides formulas for calculating the ascendant (lagna) for both day and night births using the tropical sun's position.</summary>

# Page 2
## 9

The following is an example of calculating the remaining portion to be traversed <note>Sanskrit: *bhogya-sādhya*, referring to the portion of a zodiac sign yet to rise above the horizon</note>. In the place of birth, the Sun's position is 7 signs, 30 degrees, and 6 minutes. When added to the precession of the equinoxes <note>Sanskrit: *ayana-amśa*, the longitudinal difference between the tropical and sidereal zodiacs</note> of 18 degrees and 56 minutes, the result is 12 degrees, 25 minutes, and 56 seconds <note>within the sign of Capricorn</note>. The remaining degrees of this sign are 4 degrees, 4 minutes, and 54 seconds. The rising time <note>Sanskrit: *udaya*</note> for the sign of Capricorn is 298. This is multiplied by the remaining degrees, resulting in 1211, 122, and 22. When divided by 30 <note>the total degrees in a sign</note>, the "time to be traversed" <note>Sanskrit: *bhogya-kāla*</note> is 40;22,44. This is subtracted from the desired time <note>Sanskrit: *ishta-nāḍī*, the time elapsed since sunrise</note> of 12 hours, 18 minutes, and 30 seconds (converted to 732;30 units), leaving a remainder of 692;6,16. Following Capricorn, the rising time of Aquarius is 239; subtracting this from the remainder leaves 452;7,16. Following Aquarius, the rising time of Pisces is 203; subtracting this leaves 259;7,16. Following Pisces, the rising time of Aries is 203; the remainder is—

-> **Table of Rising Times at Local Latitude** <-

| Sign | Rising Time | Sign |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Aries | 203 | Pisces |
| Taurus | 221 | Aquarius |
| Gemini | 248 | Capricorn |
| Cancer | 248 | Sagittarius |
| Leo | 251 | Scorpio |
| Virgo | 252 | Libra |

—from the remainder, 56;7,16. Following Aries, the rising time of Taurus is 239. Since the remainder 53;27,16 <note>corrected from 56;7,16</note> cannot be subtracted from this, the remainder 53;7,16 is multiplied by 30, giving 1593;7,28. This is divided by the "undigested" rising time <note>the sign that did not fully rise</note> of 239, yielding 6 degrees, 40 minutes, and 4 seconds. This is added to the signs already fully passed, resulting in 6;40,4. When the precession of 18;56 is subtracted, the **Ascendant (Lagna)** is determined to be 7 signs, 18 degrees, and 44 minutes. By adding six signs, the **Seventh House (Descendant)** is found to be 6 signs, 18 degrees, and 44 minutes.

Now, for the example of the **Tenth House (Midheaven)**. Here, we use the rising times at the Equator <note>Sanskrit: *lankodaya*, literally "rising times at Lanka," the theoretical city on the equator</note>. Midday is 12;44. Subtracting the time elapsed since sunrise (12;18,30) gives a "time before noon" of 0;25,30. This is the desired time.

-> **Table of Rising Times at the Equator** <-

| Sign | Rising Time | Sign |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Aries | 278 | Pisces |
| Taurus | 299 | Aquarius |
| Gemini | 323 | Capricorn |
| Cancer | 323 | Sagittarius |
| Leo | 299 | Scorpio |
| Virgo | 278 | Libra |

Using the equatorial rising times, the degrees of the Sun already traversed are multiplied. The resulting figure is divided by thirty to find the "traversed time," which is 279;14,0. This cannot be subtracted from the time units of 25;30. Therefore, the remainder 25;30 is multiplied by thirty (765) and divided by its own rising time of 323. The result in degrees is 2;22,6. When subtracted from the Sun's position (7 signs, 30 degrees, 6 minutes), the result is 5 signs, 7 degrees, 0 minutes. Adding six signs gives the **Fourth House (IC)** at 5 signs, 8 degrees, 0 minutes. The difference for the fourth house calculation is 2;16,5,56. By adding the calculated correction of 0;12,48,59,20, the result is the **Cusp of the Ascendant** at 2;2,3,2. Now—

# Now, the Results of Planets Situated within a House
Regarding the phrase "situated between two planetary junctions": This means that when a planet's position is greater than the starting junction <term>Sandhi; the point where one astrological house ends and another begins</term> and less than the ending junction, then that planet is considered to be "within the house" and yields the full results of that house. If the planet is less than or greater than these two junctions, it yields the results of the preceding or succeeding house. Specifically, if the planet is less than the starting junction, it gives results based on the <term>Vinshopaka; a unit of twenty used to measure planetary strength</term> of the previous house. When the planet is greater than the ending junction, it gives the results of the following house. || 28 ||

Now, the author explains the house results in terms of these twenty units (Vinshopakas). The following example is explained: The Sun is at 10 signs, 7 degrees, 30 minutes, and 6 seconds.

> The result of a planet situated between two junctions belongs to that house. || If it is less or more than the two junctions, the result belongs to the preceding or succeeding house. || 28 || One should calculate the difference between the planet and the junction, multiply it by twenty (20), and divide it. || The quotient obtained by dividing by the difference between the house junctions is known as the result in Vinshopakas. || 29 || Mars, Venus, Mercury, the Moon, the Sun, Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn, Saturn, and Jupiter are the lords of the signs. || The first are the lords of the <term>Drekkana; a ten-degree subdivision of a zodiac sign</term> starting from Mars; the middle ones from the Sun; and the third from Venus, following the order of the signs. || 30 ||

---

Now, the Table of Decans (Drekkana) is written:

| Auspicious | Ar | Ta | Ge | Ca | Le | Vi | Li | Sc | Sg | Cp | Aq | Pi |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **10°** | Mar | Ven | Mer | Moo | Sun | Mer | Ven | Mar | Jup | Sat | Sat | Jup |
| **20°** | Sun | Mar | Ven | Mer | Moo | Sun | Mer | Ven | Mar | Jup | Sat | Sat |
| **30°** | Jup | Sat | Sat | Jup | Mar | Ven | Mer | Moo | Sun | Mer | Ven | Mar |

<note>The table headers use abbreviations for the zodiac signs: Aries, Taurus, Gemini, Cancer, Leo, Virgo, Libra, Scorpio, Sagittarius, Capricorn, Aquarius, and Pisces.</note>

---

The junction is 4|22|18|32. The difference between these two is 14|48|17. Multiplied by twenty (20), it is 296|5|40. The house is 5|5|8|0 and the junction is 4|22|18|32; the difference between them is 17|10|23. Now, dividing the dividend 1,029,540 by the divisor 61,823, the result obtained is the Vinshopaka value: 17|34. The same applies to the Moon and others. Thus are the house results in Vinshopakas for the Sun and other planets. || 29 ||

Now, the strength and weakness of the planets will be discussed. Because planetary strength is a vast subject, the author first states the lords of the signs and portions to calculate that strength. Regarding "Mars etc.": Mars is the lord of Aries and Scorpio...

Saturn, Venus; for Gemini, the Son of the Moon <note>original: "Saumya," an epithet for Mercury</note> is Mercury; for Cancer, the Moon-bearer <note>original: "Shashi-Chandra"</note>; for Leo, the Master <note>original: "Ina," an epithet for the Sun</note> is the Sun; for Virgo, the Wealthy <note>original: "Vitta"</note> is Mercury; for Libra, the White One <note>original: "Sita"</note> is Venus; for Scorpio, the Mars-like <note>original: "Ara"</note> is Mars; for Sagittarius, the Teacher <note>original: "Ijya"</note> is Jupiter; for Capricorn, the Son of the Sun <note>original: "Arki"</note> is Saturn; for Aquarius, the Slow One <note>original: "Manda"</note> is Saturn; for Pisces, the Eloquent <note>original: "Angira"</note> is Jupiter. These are the planetary lords who become the masters of the twelve zodiac signs.

Now, the lords of the Decans <term>Drekkana: a subdivision of a zodiac sign consisting of 10 degrees</term> are described. Regarding the "First" etc.: a third part of a sign, consisting of ten degrees, is called a Decan. In one sign, there are three Decans. Therefore, one must consider which number Decan it is within a sign. If it is the first, it is ruled by Mars; if the second, by Venus; if the third, by Mercury. In this manner, the Decan-protectors <note>original: "drekkana-pa"</note> or Decan-lords are determined for the twelve signs starting from Aries. This concludes the Decan Chart. || 30 ||

**Now, the low positions of the planets.** The author explains the removal of the high position <note>exaltation</note> and the lords of the portions. He says: "That." For those planets starting with the Sun, the seventh sign from their exaltation—beginning with Libra—is their low position. The degrees are the same. For example, the Sun's low point is in Libra at ten degrees; this is the point of deepest debilitation. This should be understood for the Moon and the others as well.

**Now, the strength of the high position.** A planet should be subtracted from its deepest low point. If the result is greater than seven signs, it should be subtracted from the full circle of twelve signs. The remaining signs should be converted to degrees and divided by nine. The resulting quotient is the "High Strength" <term>Uchcha-bala: the mathematical strength a planet gains by being near its exaltation point</term>. This concludes the High Strength.

**Now, the Ninth-Portions <term>Navamshas: a 1/9th division of a zodiac sign used for fine-tuned predictions</term> are described.** Aries is *Kriya*; Capricorn is *Ena*; Libra is *Tauli*; Cancer is *Indubha*. These are the starting points for the groups of signs beginning with Aries. Specifically: for Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius, the first ninth-portion is Aries. For Taurus, Virgo, and Capricorn, the first is Capricorn. For Gemini,

Jupiter is the lord; then for the next six parts, <term>Arphinj</term><note>A Sanskritization of the Arabic 'Afarinj', referring to Venus</note> Venus is the lord; then for the next eight parts, <term>Jña</term><note>The 'knower', a common name for Mercury</note> Mercury is the lord; then for the next five parts, <term>Ara</term><note>A name for Mars</note> Mars is the lord; then for the last five parts, Saturn is the lord. ### Now, the Five-Fold Strength (Panchavargiya Bala) is described:

In Aries, the divisions of 6, 6, 8, 5, and 5 parts are ruled respectively by Mercury, Venus, Jupiter, Mars, and Saturn. || 34 ||

In Cancer, the divisions of 7, 6, 6, 7, and 4 parts are ruled by Mars, Venus, Mercury, Jupiter, and Saturn. || 35 ||

In Leo and Capricorn, the divisions of 6, 5, 7, 6, and 6 parts are ruled by Jupiter, Venus, Saturn, Mercury, and Mars. || 35 || <note>The numbering in the original manuscript repeats 35 here.</note>

In Gemini, the divisions of 6, 7, 8, 5, and 4 parts are ruled by Mercury, Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, and the Sun. || 35 ||

In Libra, the divisions of 6, 8, 7, 7, and 2 parts are ruled by Saturn, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, and Mars. In Scorpio, the divisions of 7, 8, 8, 5, and 2 parts are ruled by Mars, Venus, Mercury, Jupiter, and Saturn. || 36 ||

In Sagittarius, the divisions of 12, 5, 4, 5, and 4 parts are ruled by Jupiter, Venus, Mercury, Mars, and Saturn. In Capricorn, the divisions of 7, 8, 8, 4, and 3 parts are ruled by Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, Saturn, and Mercury. || 37 ||

In Aquarius, the divisions of 7, 6, 5, 5, and 7 parts are ruled by Venus, Mercury, Jupiter, and Saturn. In Pisces, the divisions of 12, 4, 3, 9, and 2 parts are ruled by Venus, Jupiter, Mercury, Mars, and Saturn. || 38 ||

### The Calculation of Strength:

In one's own sign, the strength is 30; in the sign of exaltation, it is 20; in the <term>Drekkana</term><note>A ten-degree division of a sign, also known as a decan</note>, it is 10; in the <term>Musalaha</term><note>A specific planetary dignity in Tajika astrology</note>, it is 5. These are to be calculated in <term>Vinhsopaka</term><note>A unit of astrological strength based on a scale of 20</note> units divided into four parts. || 39 ||

**The Commentary states:** When a planet is in its own sign, it gains 30 points of strength. In its highest exaltation, it gains 20. In its own Hadda, it gains 15. In its own Drekkana, it gains 10. In the Musalaha, it is said to gain 5 parts. These are to be projected or calculated as Vishopakas in four ways. || 39 ||

# Page 12
## Chapter 1

When a planet is situated in its own house, it possesses twenty-five units of authority. In its own specific jurisdiction <note>original: "svasvadhikare"</note>, the strength is eighteen. In the house of a great friend <note>original: "suhrid-mitra"</note>, it is three-quarters of that, or twenty-two and a half. In a neutral house <note>original: "samabha"</note>, it is half, or fifteen. In the house of an enemy <note>original: "ari-mitra-shatru"</note>, it is one-quarter, or seven and a half. 

To illustrate: if a planet is in its own house, it has 30 units of strength. In a friend's house, it becomes twenty-two and a half (22:30). In a neutral sign, it becomes half, which is fifteen (15). In an enemy's sign, it is one-fourth, which is seven and a half (7:30). 

Similarly, in its own <term>Hadda</term> <note>A specific division of a zodiac sign, similar to Egyptian 'Terms'</note>, the strength is fifteen (15). In a friend's Hadda, it is eleven and a quarter (11:15). In a neutral Hadda, it is seven and a half (7:30). In an enemy's Hadda, it is three and three-quarters (3:45). 

Furthermore, in its own <term>Drekkana</term> <note>A 10-degree division of a sign; a decan</note>, the strength is ten (10). In a friend's Drekkana, it is seven and a half (7:30). In a neutral Drekkana, it is five (5). In an enemy's Drekkana, it is two and a half (2:30). 

Likewise, in its own <term>Navamsha</term> <note>The 9th part of a sign, crucial for determining a planet's true dignity</note>, the strength is five (5). In a friend's Navamsha, it is three and three-quarters (3:45). 

->The strength stated for one's own jurisdiction is reduced by a quarter in a friend's house, and by half in a neutral sign.<-
->Having thus calculated the strengths, their sum is divided by four; a planet with less than five units is considered weak. || 40 ||<-

In a neutral Navamsha, the strength is two and a half (2:30). In an enemy's Navamsha, it is one and a quarter (1:15). Having thus calculated the <term>Panchavargiya Bala</term> <note>The combined strength from five specific divisional charts</note>, the sum of these five divisions is divided by four. The resulting value represents the actual operational strength of the planets. If the result is less than five <note>original: "sharona," literally 'less than five'</note>, the planet is known as "weak-powered" <note>original: "hinavala"</note>. 

As it is said: "One who has less than five is weak; more than that is called middling. One with more than ten is declared truly powerful; this is the determination from the Five-Fold Strength."

Now, to demonstrate the practical application, the relationships of friend, neutral, and enemy are written. 

**Example:** The Sun is at 2 signs, 7 degrees, 30 minutes, and 6 seconds. Because it is situated in the sign of Capricorn <note>ruled by Saturn</note>, and Saturn is an enemy of the Sun, the house-strength is seven and a half (7:30). Now for the strength of exaltation <note>original: "ucchavala"</note>: The Sun's highest point of exaltation is 0 signs, 10 degrees. Subtracting this from the Sun's position...

# Page 147

Now the lords of the <term>Panchamsha</term><note>A division of a zodiac sign into five equal parts of 6 degrees each.</note> and <term>Dwadashamsha</term><note>A division of a zodiac sign into twelve equal parts of 2.5 degrees each.</note> are described. In an odd-numbered sign, the measure of a Panchamsha is six degrees (6°). Thus, there are five such divisions in a sign. In an odd sign, the lord of the first Panchamsha is Mars; the lord of the second is Saturn <note>original: "Arki"</note>; the lord of the third is Jupiter <note>original: "Ijya"</note>; the lord of the fourth is Mercury <note>original: "Jna"</note>; and the lord of the fifth is Venus <note>original: "Bhargava"</note>. In an even-numbered sign, these should be known in reverse order. That is to say: in an even sign, the lord of the first Panchamsha is Venus; the second is Mercury; the third is Jupiter; the fourth is Saturn; and the fifth is Mars.

Now the lords of the Dwadashamshas are stated. The Dwadashamshas are remembered as starting from the sign itself <note>original: "svabhat," meaning the count begins from the zodiac sign the planet currently occupies</note>. To explain: the measure of a Dwadashamsha is two and a half degrees (2° 30'). Thus, in a sign, there are twelve.

---

->In an odd sign, the lords of the fifth-parts are Mars, Saturn, Jupiter, Mercury, and Venus.<-
->In an even sign, they are known in reverse. The twelfth-parts are remembered as starting from the sign itself.<-
->|| 43 || The planet is reduced <note>converted to degrees</note>; then multiplied by the limbs (6), mountains (7), treasures (8), numerals (9), directions (10), and Rudras (11); divided by the sky-Ramas (30).<-
->The results are the logic (6), mountain (7), treasure (8), numeral (9), direction (10), and sky (12) divisions. When increased by one, they are counted from Aries.<-
->|| 44 ||<-

---

These divisions occur as follows: by this measurement, whatever number the division is, the lord of that division is the lord of the sign found by counting that many places from the planet's own sign. || 43 ||

Now the method for bringing forth <note>calculating</note> the sixth, seventh, eighth, ninth, tenth, eleventh, and twelfth divisions is stated. "The planet is reduced": the planet's position is simplified. For example, the signs are multiplied by thirty and added to the degrees below them; thus the planet's position is made into a total number of degrees. This is placed in six locations. In sequence, these are multiplied by the "limbs" (6), "mountains" (7), "treasures" (8), "numerals" (9), "directions" (10), and "Rudras" (11). There, they are divided by "sky-Ramas" (30). The quotients obtained are the divisions of logic (6), mountains (7), treasures (8), numerals (9), directions (10), and sky (12). These, when "increased by one" (added to 1), and counted starting from Aries <note>original: "Kriya"</note>, the lord of the sign where the count stops is known as the <term>Vargesha</term><note>The presiding planetary lord of that specific divisional chart.</note>. If the result after adding one is greater than twelve, it should be "purified" by twelve (12) <note>divided by 12 to find the remainder</note>. For example: the Sun is at 4 signs and 7 degrees...

# Chapter 1

The portions <note>original: "bhagas," referring to the degrees or divisions of a sign</note> of the planets present at the time of the Ascendant <note>original: "Lagna," the zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon</note> must be understood by the wise. The divisions of the benefic planets <note>the "gentle" planets like Jupiter or Venus</note> are considered best and are known to yield auspicious results. The divisions of the malefic planets <note>the "harsh" planets like Mars or Saturn</note> are not best; they are known to be unfavorable and yield wicked results. || 41 ||

Now, the lords of the *Hora*, *Drekkana*, and *Turyamsha* <note>divisional charts representing 1/2, 1/3, and 1/4 of a sign respectively</note> are described. First, the lords of the *Hora* are stated:

**In an odd sign** <note>Aries, Gemini, Leo, etc.</note>, the half of the house consists of fifteen degrees. Within that measurement, one should consider the Sun as the lord of the *Hora*. Specifically, in an odd sign, the Sun rules the first fifteen degrees, and the Moon rules the following fifteen. Likewise, **in an even sign** <note>Taurus, Cancer, Virgo, etc.</note>, one should consider the Moon and the Sun <note>meaning the Moon rules the first half and the Sun the second</note>.

Now the lords of the *Drekkana* <note>the 1/3rd division, or decanate</note> are spoken of. A *Drekkana* is known to be a third part of a sign, measuring ten degrees. If the *Drekkana* being considered falls within the first division, then the lordship of that *Drekkana* belongs to the ruler of that very sign. If it is the second *Drekkana*, the lordship belongs to the ruler of the fifth sign from that sign. Likewise, if it is the third *Drekkana*—

-> In an odd sign, the Sun and Moon; in an even sign, the Moon and Sun are the lords of the *Hora*. The lords of the *Turyamsha* are the rulers of the centers starting from one's own sign. <-
-> The lords are the rulers of the sign itself, the fourth, the seventh, and the tenth. || 42 || <-

—then the lordship of that *Drekkana* belongs to the ruler of the ninth sign from that sign.

Now the lords of the *Turyamsha* <note>the 1/4th division</note> are spoken of: "The rulers of the centers <note>original: "Kendra," the 1st, 4th, 7th, and 10th positions</note> of one's own field." The lords of the four divisions are the rulers of the centers produced from the sign occupied. For example: a sign has thirty degrees; its fourth part <note>Turyamsha</note> is seven and a half degrees (7°30'). Within the first division (up to 7°30'), the lordship belongs to the ruler of the sign itself. In the second division (up to 15°0'), the lordship belongs to the ruler of the fourth sign from one's own sign. In the third division (up to 22°30'), the lordship belongs to the ruler of the seventh sign from one's own sign. In the fourth division (up to 30°), the lordship belongs to the ruler of the tenth sign from one's own sign. || 42 ||

***

<margin>1. In an odd sign, the Sun and Moon are the lords of the halves; in an even sign, the Moon and Sun are the lords of the halves.</margin>
<margin>2. These refer to the first, fourth, seventh, and tenth positions.</margin>
